질문 내용 
1) 준공 이후 램프업 소요 기간 28 년 5 월 준공 이후 실질적 운영은 6 월 시작 
  28 년 30%, 29 년 60% 30 년 100% 가동 목표 
  4Q28 신공장 첫 엔진 생산 가능 예상 
2) 데이터센터향 엔진 수주 인콰이어리 지속 증가 추세, 8/10 수주 이후에도 추가 증가 중 
  인콰이어리가 모두 수주로 이어지진 않지만 증설 결정에는 충분한 수요라는 판단 
3) 발전용 엔진 인콰이어리 믹스 신규 인콰이어리 기준 육상용과 FDC 향 6:4~5:5 비율 
4) 경쟁사 대비 캐파 및 라인업 비교 바르질라, 에버런스가 발전용 엔진 시장 상위기업 
  양사 합산 증설 규모 4.5GW 추정,  
  경쟁사와의 라인업은 동사와 유사, 대출력 엔진 9MW, 20MW 급 위주 
5) 공장별 혼용 생산 가능 여부 기존 울산 공장 테스트 베드는 3-5MW 급 선박용에 최적화로 선박용 생산에 집중 계획 
  영암 공장 및 신규 공장은 모든 종류 엔진 생산 가능 
6) 바르질라 AM 사업과의 수익성 비교 바르질라와 유사하게 LTSA(장기유지보수계약) 구조로 진행될 가능성 높음 
  수익성 역시 바르질라와 유사한 수준 기대

자료: HD현대중공업, 상상인증권

HiMSEN 엔진 및 SMR 주기기 생산설비 대규모 증설 발표 
- 9/10 HD현대중공업은 총 1조 722억원 규모의 시설투자 2건을 공시 
① 대출력 발전엔진(4행정 중속엔진, HiMSEN엔진) 생산능력 확충에 8,336
억원 투입, 글로벌 전력수요 증가에 따른 엔진발전 시장 확대에 대응하는 목
적. 대출력 발전엔진 전용 공장 신설과 토지 취득, 건축물·기계설비 등 생산설
비 구축이 골자. 투자기간은 ‘26.09~’28.05(설계/인허가 ~’27.02, 공장건설 
~’28.05. ’28.06 상업운전개시, 28년 가동률 30%, 2030년 100% 도달) 
② SMR 주기기 제작 생산시설 구축에 2,386억원(자기자본 대비 2.55%) 투
입, SMR 주기기 제작사업 진입 및 수주 물량 증가에 대응. SMR 전용 공장 
및 설비 신설과 기존 공장 최적화를 병행, 투자기간은 ’26.10~’29.04. 
기다리던 증설 공시. 늘어난 Capa로 AI DC향 수혜 확대 기대 
- 25년 3.0GW(선박용 2.3GW + 육상용 0.7GW)였던 HiMSEN 엔진 생산 
Capa는 2030년 7.2GW(선박용 3.2GW + 육상용 4.0GW)로 확대. 그간 선
박용 중심이었던 엔진 사업의 포트폴리오를 발전용으로 본격 다각화하는 사
업 재편으로 해석 
- AI DC 신규 증설 확대 추세 속 계통 연결의 어려움과 CCGT, Aero -
derivative 대비 납기 및 부하상승시간, 모듈화 용이성 영역에서 장점을 가진 
발전용 중속엔진에 대한 수요 지속 기대(260908 당사 보고서 참조) 
- 지난 Corban Energy Group 향 계약 건(1,000MW, $ 674 mn)을 단순 
적용 시 금번 증설의 향후 매출액 기여규모는 연간 $ 2.8 bn에 달함. 엔진 시
장의 Seller-Market 기조에 따라 20%를 상회하는 높은 이익률이 지속될 것
으로 전망함에 따라 향후 유의미한 이익 성장 기대 
- HD현대마린솔루션 또한 LTSA 수주 저변 확대 측면에서 향후 이익 확장 가
능. HiMSEN엔진 고유 라이선스 보유에 따른 시너지로 그룹사 전반의 수혜 
기대

TUẦN 12-16/10 [Điểm nhấn thị trường] 
VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -29.18 điểm tương ứng -1.68%, với thanh 
khoản suy giảm

[Nhận định xu hướng] 
VN-Index có thể hồi kỹ thuật từ cạnh dưới tam giác, song việc chớm thủng hỗ trợ 
1.710 điểm và số mã còn giữ xu hướng tăng từ đáy tháng 7 suy gi ảm khiến xu 
hướng ngắn hạn trở nên trung tính.

### Page 2

2

Điểm nhấn thị trường 
Chỉ số, nhóm ngành, khối ngoại và phái sinh

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -29.18 điểm tương ứng -1.68%, với thanh khoản suy 
giảm. Dầu khí, Bảo hiểm và Dịch vụ phân phối là các nhóm ngành có mức giảm rõ rệt nhất 
trong tuần. Khối ngoại duy trì bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị -3,013 tỷ 
đồng, tập trung ở các mã MCH, VIC và FPT; mặt khác, tự doanh đảo chiều mua ròng ở mức 
887 tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại đảo chiều bán ròng -2,021 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần 
qua và đang duy trì vị thế bán lũy kế -4,384 hợp đồng. Tự doanh đảo chiều bán ròng -
4,275 hợp đồng và đang duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -7,815 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam tiếp tục điều chỉnh tuần thứ tư liên tiếp, khi VN-Index lùi về đóng cửa 1.706 
điểm - giảm khoảng 1,7% so với tuần trước và đang dao động quanh vùng hỗ trợ tâm lý 
1.700 điểm, cũng là vùng đáy cũ của tháng 8. Thanh khoản thị trường tiếp tục suy yếu, 
phản ánh tâm lý thận trọng cao độ trong bối cảnh thị trường thiếu vắng thông tin hỗ trợ 
trong nước, trong khi các yếu tố bên ngoài như xung đột tại Iran, và chi phí vốn toàn cầu 
chưa hạ nhiệt tiếp tục đè nặng tâm lý nhà đầu tư. Khối ngoại duy trì chuỗi bán ròng kéo 
dài nhiều phiên liên tiếp, nối dài đà rút vốn đã lên tới xấp xỉ 95 nghìn tỷ đồng từ đầu năm. 
Trong một, hai tuần tới, mùa báo cáo kết quả kinh doanh quý 3 được kỳ vọng là chất xúc 
tác giúp thị trường giao dịch cân bằng trở lại.

F I R S T  T O  M A R K E T

Chỉ số VNINDEX

Source: Trading View, KB Securities Vietnam

Ngắn hạn 
(Dưới 3 tháng)

Trung hạn (Từ 3 đến 9 tháng)

Chỉ báo  Nhận định xu hướng Hành động 
Xanh đậm  Khả năng tăng điểm cao Mua mạnh 
Xanh nhạt  Khả năng tăng điểm vừa phải Mua từng phần 
Vàng  Xu hướng trung tính Mua/Bán trading

Đỏ  Khả năng giảm điểm cao Bán mạnh

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX tiếp tục hình thành mẫu nến giảm điểm tiêu cực trên đồ thị tuần, đánh dấu tuần 
giảm thứ 4 liên tiếp, cho thấy áp lực phân phối vẫn đang có phần lấn át. Trên khung ngày, 
tuy chỉ số đã có nỗ lực phục hồi trở lại trong 3 phiên đan xen, đặc biệt là phản ứng trong 
phiên cuối tuần khi chạm trendline trung hạn, nhưng việc chỉ có thể hình thành các mẫu 
nến "spinning" hàm ý đà tích lũy chưa thực sự thuyết phục. Trong một vài phiên kế tiếp, 
VNINDEX có thể xuất hiện nhịp hồi kỹ thuật từ vùng hỗ trợ, tương ứng với cạnh dưới tam 
giác, nhưng sau khi chớm phá vùng hỗ trợ quanh 1710 và hiện chỉ còn một số ít các mã giữ 
được xu hướng tăng tính từ đáy tháng 7, xu hướng ngắn hạn đang trở nên khá trung tính.

(Ghi chú: NĐT có thể dựa theo nhận định xu hướng thị trường để tham khảo khuyến nghị 
hành động tương ứng trong bảng phía trên) 
Kháng cự xa: 1780 (+-15) 
Kháng cự gần: 1740 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ gần: 1680 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ xa: 1630 (+-15)

### Page 4

4

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu

Phương pháp tiếp cận: 
- Danh mục tập hợp các cổ phiếu tiềm năng nhất được KBSV 
lựa chọn và tái cơ cấu theo tháng. 
- Ngưỡng cắt lỗ cần được cân nhắc cho mỗi mã là -15%. 
- Danh mục giả định luôn duy trì tỷ trọng 100% là cổ phiếu với 
trọng số được phân bổ đều giữa các mã.

Lưu ý:  
- NĐT được khuyến nghị mua tại mức giá bằng hoặc thấp hơn 
giá khuyến nghị mua. 
- Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng được tính theo giá khuyến nghị mua. 
 So sánh hiệu suất với VN30 Index

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu 
Mã Giá đóng cửa tuần Biến động từ đầu tháng Giá khuyến nghị mua Giá mục tiêu Lợi nhuận  kỳ vọng Điểm nhấn đầu tư Báo cáo phân tích 
FRT 141.00 -4.1% 143.40 165.00 15.1% 
- Sau khi phủ sóng các thành phố lớn, chuỗi Long Châu đang mở rộng sang các vùng cấp 2, 3. 
Năm 2026 dự kiến mở thêm 400-450 cửa hàng (+20%YoY) với DT trên mỗi cửa hàng cũ tăng 
trưởng 6%-8%/năm 
- Dư địa lấy thêm thị phần từ các cửa hàng nhỏ lẻ vẫn còn tương đối rộng, FRT đặt mục tiêu 
chiếm 50% thị phần doanh thu ngành bán lẻ dược phẩm, từ mức 17%-20% hiện tại 
- Tiềm năng dài hạn từ mảng tiêm chủng tương đối tích cực 
FRT

MACRO STRATEGY
The Five Shocks of 2026—And the Extraordinary Resilience of the U.S. Economy
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
This is the year the consensus got mugged.
Rewind to the start of the year. The market consensus was that the U.S. economy was
set to slow. The Fed was set to cut interest rates. Corporate earnings were set to grow—

but not explode. Energy prices would remain tame. And the odds looked good that the
Republicans would control the House, the Senate, or both, following the midterm election.
Nine months on, the landscape looks starkly different. Defined by five shocks—energy,
interest rates, earnings, growth, and politics—2026 looks nothing like the year investors
thought they were buying on January 1.
Shock 1: Diesel Matters. While global oil prices have moved higher since the start of

the U.S./Iran war on February 28, oil prices have yet to surpass their previous record high
because the world has released some 400 million barrels from strategic reserves this year,
China has curbed its demand for oil imports, and the U.S. has goosed its energy exports to
record highs.
But here is the problem: While gasoline powers the American consumer, diesel powers the
American economy—and diesel prices are now sitting at record highs. Diesel supplies have

been hammered by wars in the Middle East and Ukraine that have sharply reduced refining
capacity. According to the International Energy Agency, by August, exports of diesel from
refiners in the Middle East had fallen by a quarter from prewar levels. Meanwhile, Ukraine
has hit 24 of Russia’s 34 largest refineries, reducing supplies from one of the world’s
largest diesel exporters. The upshot: U.S. diesel prices have soared from $3.55 a gallon
at the start of the year to roughly $6.52 a gallon. While crude oil trades for around $100

a barrel, the retail equivalent of a barrel of diesel now costs more than $260. The diesel
shock of 2026 was the last thing investors saw coming—and remains a key impediment to
structurally lower inflation.
Shock 2: From Interest Rate Cuts to Rate Hikes. Perhaps no other consensus view
entering 2026 has been turned more dramatically on its head than expectations for the
Fed. Heading into 2026, markets were pricing in rate cuts on expectations of a cooling

labor market, moderating inflation, and slower economic growth. The Fed easing cycle was
expected to continue in 2026. In January 2026, futures showed a roughly 50% chance of a
cut by March.
Expectations, however, flipped in late February with the start of the U.S./Iran war. The
Middle East energy shock reignited inflationary pressures, which, combined with stronger-
than-expected U.S. economic growth and a tightening labor market, have demolished the

odds of easier money in 2026. Short- and long-term Treasury yields rose sharply last week
in the wake of the S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI)
which pointed to the strongest business expansion in more than five years and the fastest
employment growth in over four years, while also reinforcing expectations that the Fed
will maintain a restrictive policy stance for longer. Markets were expecting two to three
quarter-point rate cuts in 2026; instead, the Fed has already raised rates once this year and

is expected to keep raising rates over the near term.
Shock 3: The Earnings Boom No One Saw Coming. Not all the shocks of 2026 are
negative. Indeed, stronger-than-expected corporate earnings have been among the most
positive factors of this year. Corporate America entered the year facing questions about
tariffs, AI-related capital expenditure spending, the health of the U.S. consumer, operating
leverage and the U.S. dollar outlook. At the start of the year, Wall Street was expecting

roughly $290 of S&P 500 earnings per share for 2026, per Bloomberg estimates. But by
September 21, the estimate had risen by $50, to $340 per share.
Investment Implications
While some of these shocks may
contribute to periods of market
volatility, the combination of strong
earnings growth, robust domestic
demand, and ongoing capital
investment suggests that the
fundamental backdrop for Equities
remains constructive.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Earnings have been upgraded and underpinned by several factors, including robust final 
domestic demand in the U.S., a pickup in global growth, AI-related capital spending, 
productivity gains, and enhanced operating leverage. All these variables have helped deliver 
an earnings “shock” to Wall Street. And as an aside, yes, headline profit growth has been 
inflated by strong technology earnings and one-off investment gains from Amazon and 
Alphabet. That said, the S&P 500’s earnings story remains extraordinary. According to 
FactSet, the 493 companies excluding the Magnificent 7 1 
posted 31.8% earnings growth in 
Q2, the strongest level since late 2021.

Shock 4: An Economy with more Horsepower than the Headlines Suggest. That 
corporate profits have exceeded expectations this ye
ar is due in no small part to an economy 
that continues to surprise to the upside. The Fed was supposed to cut rates this year to 
keep the economy humming and prevent it from losing steam. But rather than limp through 
2026, the economy has leaped ahead. Yes, real gross domestic product (GDP) growth of 
2.1% in Q1 and 1.5% in Q2 are hardly blockbuster annualized rates of growth. However, the 
figures belie the underlying strength of the U.S. economy, with real final domestic demand

(which strips out trade) running at a 4.2% annual rate in Q2. According to the latest reading 
of the Atlanta Fed GDP tracker, Q3 real GDP grow
th is running at a red-hot rate of 5.1%. 
Slowdown? What slowdown?
Resilient consumer spending, robust capital investment, record leve
ls of new business 
formation, and strong foreign direct investment inflows have combined to stoke growth 
in the $32 trillion behemoth called the U.S. economy—the most resilient and dynamic 
economy in the world.
Shock 5: The Unexpected Washington Reset. The last shock is still in the making, but 
given current trends, the odds are rising that the Democrats could control both the House 
and Senate follow
ing the midterm elections. To this point, in January, and per Kalshi, the 
Democrats had a 71% shot at the House and an 18% chance of taking the Senate. Fast-
forward to today, and the odds are a 90% chance that the Democrats take the House and a 
60% chance that they take the Senate.
How all of this plays out remains to be seen—there are some 36 days before the November 
vote, and anything can happen. The market base case remains that the Democrats take the 
House, and the Senate is a toss-up—or too close to call. But with the Democrats expected to 
control at least one chamber of Congress, inv
estors can expect more volatility and uncertainty 
about negotiations ov
er the budget, debt ceiling, defense spending and AI regulation. Another 
political wildcard to watch: the changing dynamics within the Democratic Party.
The Biggest Shock of All for 2026? Pe
rhaps it’s not $6 diesel, rising interest rates, 
solid growth, booming profits or shifting politics. Rather, it’s the ability of the U.S. 
economy to absorb the unexpected, not buckle under stress, and continue generating  wealth 
for investors. To wit, the S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth
 consecutive year of double-digit 
returns.

MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has
2 Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics. Data through August 31, 2026.
3 U.S. Treasury, Bureau of Economic Analysis. Data as of September 21, 2026.
4 Bloomberg, data through December 31, 2022.
Investment Implications
In our view, any related market
weakness could represent a

potential buying opportunity for
long-term investors.

### Page 5

5 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.

THE TICKER TAPE
What is the bond market telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
We believe the bond market is telling us that the Fed is on track to meeting their dual
mandate of price stability and full employment. More importantly, when examining the
full yield curve the 2-year Treasury yield is currently around 4.88%, which typically is
the most sensitive to the fed funds rate and tends to price in future potential policy

adjustments. At the same time, the 10-year Treasury yield is currently about 5.11%. This
leads us to believe that the market expects the Fed to engineer a couple of more hikes
while the back end of the curve is indicating that economic growth is still healthy.
Taken together, it appears the full curve, which has clearly flattened, believes the Fed is
doing its job, while supply shocks, for obvious reasons, are not leading to higher long-run
inflation. The backup in long-dated yields is growth, not necessarily inflation as indicated

by rising “real” yields. Therefore, we view the drift higher in yields as a more normal sign
than what many have originally suggested. We continue to emphasize higher economic
growth for longer, rising productivity in the medium term, supply shortages to remain in
key areas within the AI space, and shocks in the energy equation. We expect the energy
shocks to ultimately fade away over time, but we do not believe that growth fades
anytime soon. In our view, a more normal bond market pricing in Fed moves at the short

end, and growth at the long end helps financial conditions and economic growth operate
in a more durable manner.
In summary, the front-end of the curve expects 3 to 4 more hikes in the next 18 months
or so. This can be debated obviously based on future data especially short-term energy
supply shocks. Long-end yields are drifting higher based on five main factors below, in
our view.
1. Strong economic growth (both
services and manufacturing)
2. Hawkish Fed

3. Debt and deficit concerns
4. Lower demand for long duration
sovereign debt in general
5. Energy supply shocks
The largest weight being strong economic activity which kick starts portfolio
repositioning particularly as the Fed hikes rates. The repositioning stops once demand
rises again when yields reach attractive levels for the more patient longer term
investment community. The supply shocks ultimately end and debt and deficit worries will
likely remain, but they still take a backseat for now. Longer duration government debt is

becoming more attractive each week even in the face of the concerns above. Be patient

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

S&P 500 Index Price Return is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States. A price return index is a stock
market index that tracks only the price changes and capital gains or losses of its component securities, without including dividends or cash distributions.

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing

3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026EDITORIAL
POSTCARD FROM BANGKOK IMF MEETINGS: DON’T TAKE RESILIENCE FOR GRANTED
1 See EcoNews for more details.
The global economy’s unexpected resilience might have invited the self-congratulation that is a trademark of internatio-
nal official meetings. But policymakers gathered in Bangkok for the 2026 IMF Annual Meetings were wearily aware that
“continued resilience cannot be taken for granted”, as noted in the prologue to the closing Chair’s Statement to the Inter-

national Monetary and Financial Committee meeting. Growth forecasts remain in line with those made a year ago, when
interest rates were much lower and no energy shock was in the cards, and even with those made in April, when a short
shock was the base case. But what has sustained global growth against the odds so far could falter before the headwinds
ease: oil buffers are being depleted; fiscal policy urgently needs to shift from support to deficit reduction as interest rates

remain persistently higher; and the AI boom that has been a salvatory growth engine comes with serious questions about
its sustainability and long-term economic effects. In addition to these global issues, the combination of fiscal challenges
and elections in France and the US were seen as key risks, while some topics that had until recently been in the spotlight
found themselves overshadowed.
BETTER GROWTH THAN EXPECTED FOR MOST , WORSE INFLATION FOR ALL

Despite the energy shock and surge in interest rates, the IMF's
latest global growth forecast is unchanged from a year ago and April
— at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4% in 2027 — although the geographical
breakdown has shifted: war-exposed countries and many energy im -
porters have been downgraded, while AI-exposed countries have been
upgraded. Asia is the standout winner, expected to account for 68% of
global growth in 2026 — a feat for a region highly dependent on Gulf

energy. Compared to July, the growth forecast for advanced economies
is revised up to 2.1% in 2026 (+0.4pp, driven by Germany) and remains
at 1.8% in 2027. Among emerging markets, the IMF upgraded Asia and
downgraded other regions. Meanwhile, global headline inflation is ex-
pected to rise to 4.7% in 2026, mainly due to energy and food, before
easing to 4.1% in 2027 (revised +0.2pp)1.
AI OPTIMISM DOMINATES, BUT WITH SOME HEAVY UNANSWERED
QUESTIONS

The topic of AI dominated the Meetings. The broad consensus was that
it is transformational and offers great opportunities to all countries.
But, for now, it is producing a mixed bag of economic effects: a power-
ful boost to global demand, directly through an investment and trade
boom and indirectly through wealth effects from stock-market gains;
but also much higher interest rates (fuelled by the borrowing needed
to fund the AI build-out, and higher prices for chips, all the products

they go into, and the electricity and water data centres need).
Beyond this shared diagnosis, strong arguments could be heard on both
sides of several critical questions. The most pressing being: will AI ge-
nerate earnings commensurate with current expectations? If not — or
even if expectations are merely downgraded — expect large declines in
AI-related asset prices, a sudden stop in funding and a significant blow
to growth wherever AI has been a key engine. This is not in anyone’s

baseline for next year, but it is clearly a top risk. To what extent will AI
boost productivity over five or ten years? The IMF incorporates almost
no boost in its five-year baseline, but presents an upside scenario in
which it does. Will AI create more jobs than it destroys, raise or reduce
labour’s share of income, prove inflationary or deflationary beyond the
near term, and strengthen cyber resilience or turbocharge attacks?
And how should it be taxed to preserve the fiscal base and offset unde-

sirable labour-market effects?
A MIXED POLICY SCORECARD
Monetary policy has appropriately taken a hawkish turn nearly eve -
rywhere, and an increase in rate rises is likely in key economies, though
not enough to threaten growth. Eurozone central bankers were parti -
cularly clear in sticking to their “moderate response” playbook.
Fiscal policy, however, has yet to adjust to the end of cheap debt. With
public debt high in most countries, a growing share of national bud -

gets will be absorbed by interest payments. Yet very few countries are
consolidating. The IMF’s top call to action was to address this urgent-
ly, especially among the worst offenders — the US and France. Fiscal
restraint would also help central banks curb inflation, reducing the
need for higher interest rates.
Policies to redress global imbalances are not closer to being adop -
ted than at previous IMF Meetings, but they were not particular -
ly highlighted by the IMF this time, beyond the mantra that “the US

needs to save more, China to consume more, and Europe to invest
more”. There were reportedly good discussions behind closed doors
— at the G7 on “non-market policies and practices (NMPPs)” — and
encouraging early steps in a long journey in Europe-China talks. That
is better than nothing, but hardly enough to expect progress within a
reasonable timeframe. Meanwhile, the risks of a disorderly unwind will
keep rising.
TWO ZONES OF CONCERN IN FOCUS: FRANCE AND THE US

France was a top concern for many participants. They feared a repeat
of the 2010–2012 Eurozone debt crisis, seeing a country with public
debt approaching 120% of GDP , sub-par growth, a poor record of fiscal
consolidation, riots in the streets and presidential elections six mon -
ths away, with populist candidates leading the polls. French and Eu -
ropean policymakers, alongside private-sector participants, presented
a different picture, namely: a fiscal situation that is serious, but is

being managed (the draft budget provides for meaningful adjustment
and is very likely to be adopted without being weakened); a widening
consensus from centre-left to far-right which favours a return to fiscal
responsibility, in line with a similar shift in public opinion; and the
electoral process is only beginning, with the outcome still wide open —
not least because the candidates’ names and numbers are not known.

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
It was also pointed out that the quantum of fiscal adjustment required
is not out of line with previous consolidation episodes in France in the
2010s and the 1990s. Still, most participants left not much reassured
and expecting a bumpy ride ahead.
US participants outside government saw a growing likelihood of a De -
mocratic sweep in the midterm elections, Either way, they expected

little fiscal consolidation, despite a manifestly challenging fiscal path
that may end up causing volatility in financial markets. Non-US parti -
cipants also feared growing policy unpredictability.
OVERSHADOWED TOPICS: THE STARS OF RECENT IMF MEETINGS
Tariffs were entirely absent from the agenda and conversations. Toke-
nised finance (rather than the narrower topic of stablecoins) still fea -
tured, but far less prominently: as the field takes shape, discussion

has shifted from the conceptual what and why to the technical how.
Climate change and low-income countries remained on the agenda,
but far less in focus than at previous Meetings. The sheer number of
issues demanding attention may be overwhelming financial policyma-
kers’ bandwidth.
*****
All in all, while the global context remains extraordinarily challenging,
it’s comforting to see that global financial policy makers are broadly
alert to both risks and opportunities, and to what needs to be done for

the latter to prevail. Now they need to deliver.
Isabelle Mateos y Lago
isabelle.mateosylago@bnpparibas.com

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?
Institutional adoption of stablecoins continues to incrementally progress in 2026. While
the frenzy from GENIUS has diminished, stablecoins’ links to the financial system have
continued to deepen, and more policymakers are seriously considering their use cases.
The market cap has hovered around ~$300bn over the past 12 months, a sign that the

stablecoin market is maturing. Stablecoin payments, while still marginal relative to
stablecoins' crypto-native purpose, are also growing, rising from around $103bn in
adjusted transaction volume in 2022 to $375bn in 2026 YTD.
For now, institutional adoption remains concentrated in cross-border payments, treasury
management, 24/7 liquidity provision, collateral, and real-time settlement.
The next big question is whether stablecoins will evolve to become merely a payments

technology, or a genuine disruptor to the global banking system. As we previously
established, the US promotion of stablecoins remains a geopolitical tool to ensure USD
dominance. Bloomberg also reported that the Trump administration is considering joint
ventures with private actors to promote USD stablecoins overseas, in a bid to boost the
dollar’s dominance and source demand for US Treasuries. Last week, Circle announced
plans to expand into 40 new markets in Southeast Asia, LatAm and the Middle East. 99%

of stablecoins (by market cap) remain pegged to USD and are largely backed by US
Treasuries.
Looking ahead, the key debate to watch is whether US regulations will allow for yield-
bearing stablecoins. Research shows that consumers say policymakers should prohibit
crypto companies from offering interest-like rewards for holding stablecoins, which will
threaten to siphon off the bank deposits that drive local lending. The US CLARITY Act
failed in Sep after lawmakers failed to agree on whether the broader crypto market

structure would support such yield-bearing stablecoins.
In this chartbook, we revisit the stablecoin market a year after the GENIUS Act was
signed. We look at: (i) how adoption has evolved, (ii) the emergence of stablecoins as
financial market plumbing via liquidity provisioning and collateral management, and (iii)
the key regulatory debates that will determine the industry’s next direction.
Payment volume has a +66% CAGR

REFRESHER: What are stablecoins?
– Definition: Digital tokens pegged to “stable” assets (e.g. USD or gold), issued
and transferred on a blockchain, which records ownership and transactions.
– Fiat-backed stablecoins are considered fully “collateralized”: USD
stablecoin with 1mn coins in circulation would be backed by $1mn in
cash and equivalents held in reserve by the stablecoin issuer.

– Types: Fiat-, commodity-, crypto- and algorithmic-backed.
– ~99% of stablecoins market valuation is pegged to the USD.
– Market cap: $5bn (2020) to ~$310bn (2026)
– Concentrated in USDT (~60%) and USDC (~23%).
– Benefits: 24/7 transfers & settlement, lower financial intermediaries,
programmability (e.g. escrow, conditional release, automated reconciliation),
and dollar-based store of value.
– Use cases:*
– Crypto trading (~77%) remains dominant.

– Payments (5-12%) growing, cross-border transfers and remittances.
– Store of value (17-33%) especially in emerging markets.
– Other: (~2%-8%): on/off ramps and tokenized RWA settlement.
– Alternatives: CBDCs, tokenized deposits, traditional financial architecture
*Estimates on market share taken from BCG analysis.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg Finance LP. Shows stablecoin total
market cap. Updated October 192026.
Stablecoins’ reached a plateau of ~$310bn in October 2026

ADOPTION OVERVIEW: Most stablecoin volume is still not driven by real economic
activity
Stablecoin transaction volume:
– ~$100trn (unadjusted), ~$2.42trn (adjusted) in 2026 YTD, according to
Allium.
– Chainalysis places more bullish estimates of $28trn in 2025; projected to
reach $719trn by 2035 on organic growth alone.
Stablecoin payments:
– Stablecoin payment volume grew from $151bn in 2022 to $375bn 2026 YTD

(according to VisaOnChain Analytics).
– Businesses account for the majority (58%-64%) of payments, led by B2B
payment volumes.
– Allium Sep report showed largest business payment categories include
service fees ($56bn), payroll ($43bn) and service payments ($32bn).
– Case study: Payments volume across Visa’s >160 stablecoin-linked card
programs across consumer, business and commercial activity grew ~200%
y/y.
Strongest adoption in remittances, B2B payments and dollar access in countries

with unstable currencies or weak banking systems.
– Benefits for cross-border use cases: Instant settlement, <$0.10 per
transaction, borderless, transparent, 24/7, no intermediaries. Remittance fees
are lower (0.5-3.0%) than the global fee average (~6.6%).
– Stablecoins represented 0.31% of fiat cross-border payment value in 2025.
Marginal portion but registered +64% growth increase vs fiat (+9%) according
to Allium. B2B payments had the highest cross-border share (43%).

– A 2025 IMF study also shows transaction flows reached  ~7.7% of GDP in
Latin America and ~6.7% in Africa and the Middle East.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Allium. Shows top 8 countries by received payments from Jan to August 2026.
Only the receiving end is attributed, so a $100 payment counts once, not twice. That attribution covers 13.5% of
stablecoin payments.

CONSUMER/RETAIL PAYMENT: Still on the periphery
Consumer payments: Not yet there
– Retail adoption is still the most distant use case, especially amongst developed-market consumer credit card spend. A recent ABA survey found that only
24% of consumers think that stablecoins could provide meaningful benefits.
– Stablecoins still look more like a debit instrument than a credit product. They do not naturally replicate the consumer valueproposition of credit cards (i.e.

rewards, revolving credit, dispute rights, chargebacks, fraud protection and convenience), so there is limited incentive for developed-market consumers to
use them.
– Limited scope for payments also evident in wallets (see chart below). Very little of the stablecoin supply is being held as balances in consumer wallets.
Still, the number of retail payments is incrementally growing, with cross-border, emerging market use cases strongest
– Retail transaction volume: $2.6bn (2020) to $71bn (2024); $66bn YTD in 2026.*

– Strongest use in: (i) economies with hyperinflation; (ii) poor banking access; or (iii) high remittance fees.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Allium data.  Shows circulating supply by holder type (Sep 2026). Categories calculated as a percentage of total circulating supply. Note that wallets account for money being held vs
payments (money moving). Business wallets in this case refer to those holding smaller balances with quick turnover (i.e. payment services, merchants, gaming), while institutional wallets hold larger balances and turn them

over quickly, i.e. financial-services firms, treasuries, and wallets. Note: *Retail sized transactions are defined as transactions of $250 or less, according to Visa OnChain Analytics.
The amount of stablecoin supply in consumer wallets stands at ~14%

CASH/LIQUDITY MANAGEMENT: Stablecoins are evolving as a major tokenized
contender
Sources: Deutsche Bank Research, taken from DB’s Tokenised money and the future of FXand Stablecoins and
agentic commerce: new rails, old frictions.
Stablecoins can help treasury management and settlement
– Stablecoins have a strong use case for settling transactions for FX,

treasury operations and collateral management i.e. intra-day repos.
– This is because stablecoins’ 24/7 settlement can address
operational frictions in cross-border transactions.
Poses significant risk to cross-border B2B transactions for banks
– Compared to traditional banks, stablecoins offer substantial relief
from transaction costs. Our equity analysts estimate total customer
processing fee savings to increase from 0 to ~$70bn in 2035, if
consumers switch from traditional banks to stablecoins.

Stablecoins is not a replacement solution to FX settlement risk
– FX settlement requires two currencies to be exchanged
simultaneously. Currently, stablecoins only serve as an intermediary
between the two currency legs, resulting in a “stablecoin sandwich”.
– Overwhelming concentration of USD-stablecoins also limits
stablecoin ability to support widespread payment-versus payment
settlement across currency pairs.
– Stablecoins also face fragmented interoperability/liquidity issues

and regulatory uncertainty across issuers/networks/jurisdictions.
– For more, read our FX report here.
Should we explain how settlement work as it is very
interesting and Rohini has a nice chart on this ?
Probably more relevant than having the stablecoin
market cap here I would say.
A more integrated post-trade process

### Page 7

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
INSTITUTIONAL ADOPTION: Corporates, card networks, and banks are integrating
stablecoins
Institutions are racing to capture stablecoin market share
– Card networks: Mastercard expanded its settlement capabilities to include
stablecoins, using USDC and Paxos-issued stablecoins.
– Institutions: Exploring stablecoins via developing in-house permissioned

blockchain experiments and/or exploring issuance of proprietary stablecoins.
– >140 companies and financial institutions helped launch Open USD
(OUSD) in Sep 2026. OUSD reached $799mn of supply in 2 weeks.
Signals shift toward consortium-backed/network-led digital dollar
models.
Are stablecoins posing a threat to traditional payment systems?
– Bullish: Stablecoins to surpass traditional rails by the 2030s (Chainalysis).
– ~17% of stablecoin-linked card volume is occurring across Visa’s

business and commercial card programs. Shows growing adoption of
stablecoins for settlement, treasury management and cross-border
commerce.
– Key concerns for banks: Fee pressure from stablecoin adoption →
bank outflows → higher reliance on wholesale funding → pressure on
net interest margins.
– Base case: However, Deutsche Bank equity analysts see more limited near-
term risk. Visa and Mastercard to remain dominant especially in advanced
markets.

– KYC, AML, sanctions screening, wallet monitoring, fraud controls and
jurisdiction-specific limits remain essential for banks.
Stablecoin transactions could match Visa and Mastercard transactions
sometime between 2031 and 2039 (Chainalysis).
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Chainalysis.

### Page 9

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
GEOPOLITICS: Why stablecoins are a "Genius" idea for the US
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, US Treasury, Tether July 2026 report. Shows Major foreign holders of Treasury Securities.
Treasury data from June 2026.
Can sustain USD dominance
– 99% of stablecoins (by market cap) are USD-pegged.
– Stablecoins function primarily as a digital version of dollars

moving over blockchain rails, extending the dollar’s reach into
new digital environments.
– Reinforces dollar savings: The more firms invoice and transact in USD,
the more they save in USD assets. Stablecoins help preserve this
feedback loop by keeping businesses and individuals in dollar
payment rails.
Can support US debt, given share held by foreigners has fallen from
>50% in early 2000s to ~30% in early 2026.
– Stablecoin issuers total holdings: ~$170bn according to Allium x

Citizen.
– Tether: ~$141bn in direct and indirect exposure to US T-bills.
– Impact: BIS shows how $3.5bn inflow into dollar-backed stablecoins
lowers 3m T-bill yields by 2.5-3.5bp under normal conditions. Effect
more than doubles in periods of T-bill shortages.
– Forecasts: T-bills holding by stablecoin issuers could reach ~$1trn by
2028 (Treasury forecasts).
– Recent Fed SF research places it closer to $400bn by the end
of 2030, lower than the US government’s financing needs.

Stablecoin issuers’  demand for US debt could counterweight declining demand from China
Tether competes with Saudi Arabia and South Korea as major holders of US Treasuries
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco, US Treasury Data. Shows Treasury
holdings, stablecoin issuers vs China.

### Page 10

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
US GENIUS ACT: Debate on rules around payment stablecoins one year on
GENIUS provisions
– Strict issuer requirements: Issuers can be private, non-bank entity, but must
meet strict federal or state requirements.
– Reserves: Requires 1:1 backing with US cash, 93 days T-bills, repos/reverse
repos, or money market funds holding those assets.
– AML/KYC/BSA compliance obligations to stablecoin issuers, aligning them

exams, and strong internal controls.
Next steps
– GENIUS takes effect no later than 18 Jan 2027, or 120 days after final
implementing regulations are issued.
– Ongoing pushback from US banks as to whether GENIUS ban on yield-
bearing stablecoins should extend to affiliate or third-party arrangements,
given many users do not hold stablecoins directly with the issuer.
– CLARITY ACT failed in Sep because of disagreements on how
exchanges/broader crypto market structure should handle stablecoins.

– The US Treasury has proposed implementing rules for payment stablecoins.
There are two ways stablecoin yield can be paid; ongoing debate over the three-
party model
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Congressional Research Service.

RISKS: Still hotly discussed amongst policymakers and financial institutions
Bank liquidity risk
Loss of stable funding source for banks from deposit outflow (not offset by
partial flowback from stablecoin issuers’ reserve deposit)
System stability risk
Stablecoins can fail to maintain their $1 value during times of stress. Evidence shows
that USDC and Tether actually trade within 1% of their dollar value but prices can

diverge sharply during stress episodes.
Run on stablecoin issuer(s) in market stress / trust scenario triggering fire sales of
reserve assets and/or deposit withdrawals.

Framework
US

EU
Markets in Crypto-Asset Regulation
(MiCAR)
Backing Assets*
• 100% backing assets in USD cash or short-term
Treasuries (≤93-day T-bills).
• Non-significant EMTs –At least 30% reserves in
same-currency bank deposits, remainder in HQLA

• Significant EMTs - At least 60% of the reserves in
bank deposits; remainder in HQLA
• Systemic stablecoins – 30% unremunerated BoE
deposits vs 70% in UK sovereign debt (HQLA)
• Non-systemic – Full 1:1 backing in HQLR assets,
minimum 5% in on demand deposits.
UK
Multi-issuance
Stance
• Rules are still being fine-tuned; no specific focus
yet on multi-issuance.
• Multi-issuance under active review; EC considers multi-
issuance permissible, an equivalence-based framework for

EU/third-country arrangements is under legislative
consideration.
• BoE considers multi-issuance unsuitable for systemic
UK use. Preference for single-issuance models backed
by single reserve (incl. Non-GPB denom. stablecoins) .
UK Cryptoasset Regime (FCA)
UK Systemic Stablecoins Regime (BoE)
Eligible Issuers
• Bank subsidiaries, federal nonbank licensees, or
state issuers with federal registration, with
enhanced oversight for >$10bn circulation.

• EMTs: EU-authorised credit institutions or authorised
electronic money institutions. Restrictions apply to
non-EU currency EMTs (exceeding MiCAR
thresholds) as a means of exchange
• FCA-authorised firms for non-systemic stablecoins,
• Systemic issuers to be designated by HM Treasury
and jointly regulated under BoE/FCA oversight
Balance Sheet
Treatment
• Stablecoins treated as reserve-backed digital
payment assets, segregated in bankruptcy-remote

accounts and treated as cash equivalents
• EMTs are fiat-referenced cryptoassets subject to
MiCAR and e-money requirements
• (OCC/FDIC) Mandates prudential capital buffers
tailored to issuer risk profile.
• (Fed) Framework consistent with bank requirements
• EMI issuers: minimum own funds of 2% of EMT
supply; 3% for significant EMTs. Significant issuers
subject to enhanced risk-management requirements
• Systemic: 6 months operating expenses/largest

plausible loss event
• Non-systemic: 1% av. stablecoins in circulation

AUTHORS
Marion Laboure is a Managing Director and Macro Strategist at Deutsche Bank in London, and a Lecturer in Economics and Finance at
Harvard University.
She was previously an economist at the IMF, the Central Bank of Luxembourg, Barclays, and the European Commission. She has twice
received the First Prize of the American Society of Actuaries, was named a rising star in finance by Revue Banque, and is recognised

among the 45 standout women in fintech globally.
She holds a Bachelor's degree in Mathematics from the University of Paris Dauphine, a Master of Research in Economics from the
London School of Economics, and a PhD in Econometrics and Quantitative Economics from the École Normale Supérieure.
She is the author of Democratizing Finance published by Harvard University Press.
Camilla Siazon is an Associate on Deutsche Bank’s Macro and Thematic team in London.

She focuses on digital assets, geopolitics, and global macroeconomic trends. Prior to Deutsche Bank, she worked at geopolitical
advisory firms Eurasia Group and Greenmantle.
She holds a dual MA/MSc in International History from Columbia University and the London School of Economics, and a BA in History
from Barnard College, Columbia University.

### Page 1

Sustainable bank bond supply is close to reaching a record in
2026, despite Europe’s ESG simpliﬁcation agenda. We expect
another increase to €100bn in 2027, supported by sustainable
lending growth, higher redemptions and resilient investor
demand
Maureen Schuller
Sustainable bank bond supply is blooming, nearing a record in 2026
Banks issued an unexpectedly large amount of sustainable debt
this year despite Europe’s ESG deregulation eﬀorts. Up to mid-

October 2026, they issued more than €86bn in sustainable bonds
across diﬀerent instrument types, up by almost €20bn from the
same period last year.
This was a surprisingly strong increase given that favourable
primary market conditions oﬀered issuers little greenium, despite
continued evidence that sustainable bonds attract higher investor
demand, as reﬂected in stronger oversubscription rates. This
suggests that the availability of sustainable assets was not a

### Page 2

meaningful constraint on banks' sustainable debt issuance, thanks
to higher redemptions and sustainable lending growth.
Issuers are also increasingly directing ESG bond issuance towards
European capital markets, often at the expense of North American
markets, reﬂecting the growing divergence in political and
regulatory priorities between the two regions.
The bulk of the YTD supply (€76.6bn) (re)ﬁnanced green assets, with
green supply up €25bn from 2025. Instead, issuance in social and

sustainability formats did not exceed €9.6bn, trailing last year’s
supply by €4.5bn. All instrument types, from secured to unsecured
to subordinated, contributed to the higher ESG supply volumes.
However, sustainable T2 and bail-in senior (senior non-preferred
and HoldCo) issuance increased particularly strongly, as the
funding cost advantage from sustainable issuance was better for
these instruments than for covered bonds and preferred senior
debt.

Issuance under the European Green Bond Standard (EuGBS) rose
from €2.5bn to almost €12bn YTD. For many banks, ICMA-aligned
green bond issuance remained the more practical option as it
allows them to ﬁnance a broader pool of assets, rather than fully
commit the bond proceeds to Taxonomy-aligned activities. This is
particularly relevant when non-EU assets are ﬁnanced, for which
the EU regulation-based do-no-signiﬁcant-harm requirements
(DNSH) are diﬃcult to apply.

That said, with the revisions to the EU Taxonomy’s technical
screening criteria around the corner (expected in 4Q 2026), we
expect more banks to inaugurate European Green Bond
Programmes, as more types of green assets will become available
due to the EU’s simpliﬁcation agenda.

What is driving growth in ESG supply in
2027?
We expect sustainable bank bond supply to reach €100bn in
FY2027, up from €93bn in FY2026 and equivalent to 22% of total
euro-denominated bank bond supply. Green issuance remains
dominant at €85bn, of which €17bn (20%) will be issued under the
EU Green Bond Standard (EuGBS). Social, sustainable and
sustainability-linked supply will total €15bn, of which 80% will be

issued in social format.
Greater availability of social assets, together with regulatory
initiatives such as the Aﬀordable Housing Act presented by the
European Commission in September 2026, should help social
issuance to gain further traction in 2027.

Sustainable loan origination will, in general, remain an important
driver of ESG supply. Higher interest rate levels will dampen lending
growth in 2027, but sustainable lending will likely be impacted to a
lesser extent.
Borrowing for energy eﬃciency improvements or with a low carbon
footprint could trim borrowing costs, while high energy prices and
costs may incentivise companies and households to move forward

with investments to reduce their energy dependence and use.
Banks’ net-zero commitments also support the continued focus on
the greening of their balance sheets.
Moreover, banks will repay €67bn in EUR sustainable debt in 2027
up from €54bn in 2026. These redemptions should release some
assets for reﬁnancing, although loan amortisation, look-back
periods and changes in use-of-proceeds criteria may prevent a full
reﬁnancing with new sustainable debt. In 2026, banks replaced

70% of their sustainable redemptions partially or fully with new
sustainable supply. A comparable trend for 2027 would already
cover close to half of our supply estimate for next year.

Besides these fundamental supply drivers, periods of geopolitical or
sovereign debt market volatility could encourage banks to apply a
green or social label to a larger share of their issuance. This may
help them broaden and diversify their investor base and support
the execution of their transactions.
EU simpliﬁcation and asset-identiﬁcation
developments
The EU’s eﬀorts to simplify ESG rules will have mixed implications

for the growth of sustainable asset pools. The reduced corporate
sustainability reporting scope may impede asset identiﬁcation,
while revisions to the Taxonomy’s technical screening criteria could
support the growth of eligible green assets.
This year’s narrowing of the scope of the EU Corporate
Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD) to companies with more
than 1,000 employees and a turnover above €450m makes it more
diﬃcult for European banks to identify assets on their balance

sheets that are suitable for sustainable bond issuance. The
simpliﬁcation of the European sustainability reporting standards
(ESRS) and the value chain cap add to this challenge.
However, the voluntary reporting standards applicable from 1
January 2027 could still provide banks with some essential data on
smaller companies in their value chain. This may include

identiﬁcation, the proposed revisions to the Taxonomy’s technical
screening criteria could support green portfolio growth once they
enter into force in early 2027. For instance, the substantial
contribution criteria for green buildings constructed before 2027
remain mostly unchanged. The criteria would also be expanded to
include buildings that achieve a reduction in primary energy

criteria, this could support the expansion of Taxonomy-aligned

green portfolios.
The technical screening criteria for buildings built after 1 January
2027 will converge more closely with the revised European Energy
Performance of Buildings Directive (EPBD 2024/1275). However, they
would already subject all new buildings to the Zero Emission
Building (ZEB) criterion and life-cycle global warming potential
(GWP) calculations ahead of the EPBD’s 1 January 2030 deadline.
Additional recommendations from the Platform on Sustainable

Finance could also make some criteria more restrictive. The
Platform has recommended introducing a stricter top-15%
benchmark for buildings constructed before 2021, based on 2023-
26 building stock data, with the threshold tightening every ﬁve
years to align with the trajectory towards net-zero by 2050. If
incorporated into the ﬁnal technical screening criteria, this

### Page 7

Regulation (SFDR) should also remain supportive of demand for
sustainable bonds, even though these revisions will probably not
apply before 2029 (i.e. two years after implementation).
The SFDR review is set to enter trilogue negotiations, with the
Council agreeing on its position in June 2026 and the Parliament
adopting its negotiating position in September 2026. The European
Commission published its ﬁrst proposals to revise the SFDR in
November 2025, providing for three new product categories to

The transition category covers products that invest in companies
or assets with a credible path towards sustainability or that pursue
certain environmental or social transition-related objectives. The
ESG basics category includes products that integrate other
sustainability considerations beyond sustainability risks in their
investment strategy. The sustainable category encompasses
products investing in companies or assets that already meet high
sustainability standards or pursue a particular environmental or

social objective.
For each category, a proposed minimum of 70% of the investments
should be made according to the respective sustainability claim.
For the transition and sustainable categories, the 70% condition
would also be met if the proportion of investments in Taxonomy-
aligned activities is at least 15%. This would support demand
among investors subject to the SFDR for bonds that ﬁnance
Taxonomy-aligned activities.
The applicable Paris-aligned benchmark (PAB) exclusions do not

apply to use-of-proceeds instruments such as European green

further growth in 2027, even as overall bank bond supply

### Page 1

• The global landscape remains complex, shaped by AI, 
energy prices and tariffs. 
• Taiwan’s AI cycle remains strong, with robust AI 
exports and investment and an intact semiconductor 
competitive moat. 
• AI is generating broader domestic spillovers, with 
wealth effects and wage growth supporting 
consumption. 
• Taiwan is cushioning the energy shock by diversifying 
energy imports and absorbing much of the increase in 
upstream costs. 
• AI-driven growth is likely to moderate from 4Q26 into 
2027, as hyperscaler  cash-flow pressures rise and 
higher global rates lift investment costs. 
• Energy remains a n inflation risk, with domestic 
electricity prices facing upward pressure after the 
November local elections. 
• GDP growth is expected to moderate to high -single 
digits from 4Q26, while we maintain our 11.6%/5.6% 
GDP forecasts for 2026/27. 
• Inflation is likely to remain above 2% in 4Q26, with our 
2.0%/2.1% CPI forecasts for 2026/27 unchanged. 
• We maintain our call for CBC to hike 12.5bp to 2.125% 
in December, amid tighter liquidity conditions. 
• Housing should recover gradually despite credit 
easing, as excess new -home inventory remains a n 
overhang.

Taiwan: AI, energy and 4Q outlook

13 October 2026

Ma Tieying, CFA                        
Senior Economist 
matieying@dbs.com

### Page 2

Taiwan: AI, energy and 4Q outlook October 13, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            2

Global backdrop

The global landscape remains complex, 
shaped by the interplay of AI, energy 
prices and tariffs, with significant 
implications for growth, inflation and 
financial conditions.

1) AI

The debate over the durability of the AI 
investment boom continues.  Tailwinds 
include technological advances, broader 
adoption, and s trategic and geopolitical 
considerations. Headwinds range from 
uncertainty over investment returns  and 
hyperscaler financing pressure to 
physical bottlenecks , safety concerns, 
and regulatory uncertainty.

Hyperscaler financing pressure has 
attracted increasing attention . In the 
early stages of the AI investment boom, 
hyperscaler capex was largely financed 
by internal cash flow. This is now 
changing, as free cash flow is expected 
to turn negative from 4Q, requiring major 
players to rely on debt issuance and 
other external financing increasingly.

At the same time, higher global interest 
rates are raising the cost of AI 
investment. The Fed resumed rate hikes 
at its September meeting, while global 
bond yields remain under upward 
pressure amid persistent inflation and 
elevated public and private borro wing 
needs. Our baseline assumes US rates 
will rise by 50bps this year and a further 
25bps in 2027 to 4.50%, with global 
long-term rates also remaining under 
pressure. This could increasingly weigh 
on the economics and pace of AI 
investment.

US-China AI competition is also drawing 
increasing attention.  Across the five 
layers of the AI ecosystem, China is 
making rapid progress in energy, 
infrastructure, AI models and 
applications, supported by its large scale, 
cost-effective domestic manufacturing 
supply chains and huge consumer 
market, which provides a wide range of 
real-world scenarios for AI adoption. The 
emergence of a more integrated and 
cost-competitive Chinese AI ecosystem 
could increasingly challe nge the US 
ecosystem, which currently operates at a 
relatively higher cost.

China, however, remains relatively weak 
in semiconductors.  Around half of 
China's domestic AI chip market still 
relies on foreign suppliers, including 
lower-end NVIDIA chips. This 
semiconductor bottleneck remains a key 
constraint on China's AI development. In 
our view, Taiwanese semiconductor 
companies' competiti ve moat therefore 
remains largely intact in the near term.

So far, there is limited evidence of a 
significant slowdown in AI investment 
or semiconductor demand.  Hyperscaler 
capex maintained robust yoy growth 
through 2Q, while Bloomberg forecasts 
growth to moderate from 95% this year 
to 39% in 2027. Similarly, global 
semiconductor sales remained strong 
through 3Q, while WSTS expects growth 
to moderate from 90% this year to 27 % 
in 2027.

2) Energy

The US -Iran conflict remains at a 
standoff, keeping the energy -price 
outlook highly uncertain.  New 
developments are likely after the US 
midterm elections in November, with 
both further escalation and de -
escalation possible. Crude oil prices have 
remained elevated at around 
USD100/bbl amid a persistent 
geopolitical risk premium . In contrast, 
refined product prices have risen more 
sharply as refining disruptions and 
tighter product logistics have amplified 
the supply shock. Natural gas prices 
have also increased sharply, reflecting 
disruptions to LNG supply and limited 
alternative shipping routes.

Taiwan has been actively diversifying 
its energy import sources to cushion the 
impact. LNG imports from Qatar have 
fallen to zero since April, while imports 
from the US and other suppliers have 
surged to offset the shortfall, helping 
maintain stable domestic electricity 
supply.

3) Tariffs

US tariff actions are likely to accelerate 
after the US midterm elections in 
November. Two major tariff measures 
remain in the pipeline. The Section 301 
forced-labor tariff was implemented in 
July, while final tariff action under the 
Section 301 overcapacity investigation, 
which covers 16 economies, remains 
pending. Meanwhile, Phase 1 of the 
Section 232 semiconductor tariffs was 
implemented in January, while Phase 2, 
covering a broader range of 
semiconductor-related products, 
remains pending.

Taiwan has secured a relatively 
favourable framework through its trade 
and investment agreements with the 
US. Taiwan signed an Agreement on 
Reciprocal Trade (ART) and an 
Investment MOU with the US earlier this 
year. The ART establishes a 15% US tariff 
rate on Taiwanese goods, while the MOU 
provides Taiwan with preferential 
treatment under Section 232, including 
for semiconductors. The ART has yet to 
enter into force, and it remains to be 
seen how the final Section 301 tariff 
burden will interact with the 15% ART 
framework. Meanwhile, major Taiwanese 
companies such as TSMC are expanding 
their US investments under the 
Investment MOU, which could support 
relatively favourable treatment for 
Taiwan when broader Section 232 
semiconductor tariffs are introduced.

Taiwan 4Q outlook

Taiwan has navigated the complex 
global environment relatively well so far 
this year, supported by robust AI exports 
and investment, diversified energy 
imports, and efforts to absorb higher 
upstream energy costs. Some 
normalisation is likely in 4Q26 –2027, as 
the AI -driven growth boost moderates 
and domestic energy prices face greater 
pressure to adjust.

1) Growth

GDP growth remains broadly on track 
with our expectations. High -frequency 
indicators suggest that GDP growth 
should remain around 12% yoy in 3Q, 
broadly in line with 12.9% in 2Q but below 
the 15.4% peak in 1Q. Growth is expected 
to moderate to a high -single-digit pace 
from 4Q onwards. We maintain our full -
year GDP growth forecasts at 11.6% for 
2026 and 5.6% for 2027.

The AI cycle remains the key driver of 
Taiwan's growth.  Information and 
communication products and electronic 
components accounted for nearly 80% 
of total exports in the first nine months 
of this year, while manufacturing 
accounts for nearly 50% of gross fixed

capital formation. From 4Q onwards, 
export growth is expected to moderate 
from exceptionally strong levels to a 
more normal but still robust pace, 
broadly following the trajectory of 
global hyperscaler capex and 
semiconductor sales. Investment is likely 
to follow a similar path.

For now, Taiwanese technology 
companies continue to report robust 
revenue growth, led by semiconductors, 
ODMs/EMS, and PCs and servers. 
Capacity utilisation at leading Taiwanese 
foundries has also continued to rise, 
suggesting that investment demand 
remains strong.

The AI boom is also beginning to 
generate broader spillovers to the 
domestic economy. The AI-driven equity 
rally has generated significant wealth 
effects, with the TAIEX up 70% year-to-
date and Taiwan becoming the world's 
fifth-largest stock market. Taiwanese 
households have benefited from the 
appreciation of financial assets, with 
equities and mutual funds accounting for 
nearly 30% of total household financial 
assets. The equity rally has also 
generated positive income effects. Wage 
growth has picked up, led by the services 
sector, particularly financial services, as 
strong earnings at financial 
institutions—especially securities 
firms—have prompted higher 
compensation to retain talent. These 
wealth and income gains should support 
household consumption, creating a 
positive feedback loop that broadens the 
benefits of the AI boom across the wider 
economy.

                                                                                                                                            7

2) Inflation

CPI inflation is also tracking broadly in 
line with our forecast. Headline CPI 
remained elevated at 2.4% yoy in 3Q, up 
from 1.2% in 1Q and 2.2% in 2Q. We 
maintain our forecast for headline CPI 
inflation to remain close to 2.5% in 4Q, 
and retain our full-year forecasts at 2.0% 
for 2026 and 2.1% for 2027.

Energy inflation remains the main 
upside risk.  The government has 
absorbed much of the increase in energy 
costs through state -owned CPC and 
Taipower, limiting the pass -through to 
domestic consumers. With losses at both 
companies widening, the government 
will need to decide whether to provide 
additional subsidies to cover the losses 
or allow further increases in domestic 
energy prices. The September re view 
postponed the adjustment to the 
December meeting, when the overall 
situation will be reassessed after the 
November local elections. Taipower's  
latest review indicated that electricity 
rates would need to rise by 12.8%. A hike 
in electricity prices could lift inflation 
expectations and generate second -
round effects, particularly as the labour 
market tightens and wage pressures 
increase.

3) Monetary policy

The central bank kept the policy 
discount rate unchanged at 2.00% at its 
September meeting, in line with our 
expectations. We continue to expect a 
modest 12.5bp rate hike at the 
December meeting, bringing the policy 
rate to 2.125%.  By then, CPI inflation is 
likely to have remained above 2% for 
around six consecutive months. The 
possibility of an increase in electricity 
prices following the November local 
elections could further lift inflation 
expectations, strengthening the case for 
a December rate hike.

Financial conditions have already 
tightened despite the absence of a 
policy rate hike.  M2 growth remained 
around 7% yoy in 3Q, well below double-
digit nominal GDP growth. At the same 
time, bank loan growth has accelerated 
to more than 10% yoy, driven by strong

### Page 8

                                                                                                                                            8

corporate credit demand, particularly AI-
related fundraising by technology 
companies. Tighter financial conditions 
are also evident in rising market interest 
rates, including commercial paper rates 
and government bond yields.

The central bank has continued to ease 
housing credit controls gradually. At its 
September meeting, it raised the LTV 
cap on second -home mortgage loans to 
70%, the second easing of housing credit 
controls this year. There is scope for 
further easing, including removing the 
LTV cap on second-home loans or raising 
the LTV caps for third - and fourth-home 
loans and high -value properties, which 
currently stand at 30%. However, the 
central bank is likely to proceed 
cautiously, given concerns that a faster 
relaxation could reignite  speculative 
demand and put renewed upward 
pressure on housing prices.

4) Housing

A gradual and modest recovery in the 
housing market is expected from 4Q26 
into 2027.  The existing -home market 
has already shown early signs of 
recovery, with Sinyi  housing prices 
returning to slightly positive yoy growth 
since 2Q. Total building transactions also 
returned to positive yoy growth in 3Q. 
The easing of credit controls should help 
reinforce this modest recovery trend.

The new -home market remains under 
pressure. The number of new units under 
construction has started to decline since 
2023, while new completions are  only 
beginning to ease this year . Demand 
remains weak, as potential buyers 
continue to wait for lower prices. Unsold 
new homes stood at 113K units at end -
2025, compared with a historical normal 
level of around 80K, implying excess 
inventory of roughly 30K units. At the 
current pace of new -home transactions, 
it would take roughly one year to 
normalize the existing excess inventory, 
and longer when factoring in the new 
supply pipeline. Some industry experts 
estimate that it could take as long as 3.8 
years digest current inventories fully 
(here).

Document 511: 事关智能制造、汽车安全、光伏新型储能...工信部最新发声

Source PDF: all report pdf/事关智能制造、汽车安全、光伏新型储能...工信部最新发声.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 14
Status: ok

### Page 1

⼯信部要求企业申报隐藏式⻔把⼿、电吸⻔等创新设计及研发测试参数，纳⼊准⼊管理，
测试验证不充分的汽⻋产品不予准⼊，可靠性测试不低于 3 万公⾥、环境适应性验证含 “ 两
冬⼀夏 ” 。同时⽀持企业建设⾼质量⼯业数据集、开发垂域⼤模型和⼯业专⽤模型，完善光
伏、新型储能产能监测预警机制。
摘要：
10 ⽉ 20 ⽇，国务院新闻办公室举⾏新闻发布会介绍 2026 年前三季度⼯业和信息化发展情况。
会上介绍，前三季度，我国装备⼯业保持向好发展态势，持续提质增效。装备⼯业 “ 压舱⽯ ” 作⽤
持续巩固。前三季度装备⼯业增加值同⽐增⻓ 7.1% ，对⼯业增⻓贡献率达 26.6% 。⾼端装备供
给能⼒增强，⼯业机器⼈、数控机床、新能源汽⻋产量分别同⽐增⻓ 29.1% 、 13.5% 、 11% 。全
国造船完⼯量同⽐增⻓ 50.7% 。下⼀步，⼯信部将持续统筹推进扩需求、优供给、强⽣态，进⼀
步巩固提升装备⼯业竞争优势。
⼯业和信息化部总⼯程师王卫明表⽰，前三季度，围绕着整治重点⾏业⾮理性竞争，综合运⽤

市场化、法治化办法，维护公平健康市场竞争秩序。经过各⽅的共同努⼒，汽⻋⾏业应付账款
电⼦凭证⽀付⽐例明显下降，国内主流光伏组件价格企稳。
坚决防范不合理创新设计和未经充分研发测试验证的汽⻋产品进⼊市场
据央视新闻，会上介绍，近年来，在各⽅⾯的团结协作、积极进取下，我国汽⻋产业技术创新
活跃，产品迭代更新速度快，企业⽣产制造能⼒和质量管理⽔平不断提⾼，中国品牌汽⻋功能
和性能不断提升。但确实也出现了部分企业跟⻛推出新产品，测试验证不充分等问题。
为进⼀步提升汽⻋产品安全技术性能，保障公共安全和公⺠⽣命财产安全，近⽇，⼯信部会同
公安部、⽣态环境部、市场监管总局等部⻔，研究编制了《关于进⼀步加强汽⻋产品创新设计
和研发测试验证有关管理⼯作的通知（征求意⻅稿）》，已向社会公开征求意⻅，重点包括三⽅
⾯：
⼀是新增相关技术参数，压实企业责任。《通知》明确要求企业填报隐藏式⻔把⼿、电吸⻔、虚
拟操纵件等创新设计和环境适应性等研发测试验证有关技术参数，并将其纳⼊⼯信部汽⻋产品
准⼊和⽣产⼀致性管理。对存在未按要求申报、测试验证不充分等问题的汽⻋产品，不予准

⼊，守牢产品安全关⼝。
⼆是加强汽⻋产品创新设计安全评估。针对异形⽅向盘、虚拟操纵件等 20 余项汽⻋产品创新设
计，《通知》提出前置安全⻛险评估等 4 项总体要求和 21 项具体要求，⽬的是督促指导企业对⻋
辆功能安全性、驾驶安全性、操控便利性、维修经济性等进⾏充分评估验证。其中，要求电吸
⻔必须具备防夹功能，禁⽌在⻋辆⾏驶状态下使⽤ “ 零重⼒ ” 座椅、旋转座椅、座椅放倒成床、升
顶座舱等功能，更好保障消费者⽤⻋安全。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

三是充分开展汽⻋产品研发测试验证。《通知》提出建⽴健全产品研发测试验证管理制度等 6 项
总体要求、 8 项具体要求，将 22 项推荐性标准、⾏业标准上升为强制性要求，督促指导企业开展
综合可靠性与耐久性等 7 ⽅⾯测试验证。其中，可靠性测试验证不低于 3 万公⾥，环境适应性测
试验证周期不少于 1 年，且包含 “ 两冬⼀夏 ” 试验。要求汽⻋⽣产企业应进⾏动⼒蓄电池循环寿
命、耐久性等实测验证，不得直接采信供应商的验证报告。
安全是汽⻋企业的⽣命线与核⼼竞争⼒，任何研发活动都要以安全为第⼀前提，绝不能以牺牲
安全为代价。下⼀步，⼯信部将积极研究吸纳各⽅⾯意⻅，会同相关部⻔加快编制印发《通
知》，加快制修订相关标准，强化产品⽣产⼀致性监督检查、检验检测机构检查，通过事前准
⼊、事中事后监督检查全链条监管，坚决防范不合理创新设计和未经充分研发测试验证的产品
进⼊市场，坚决守牢汽⻋产品安全底线，切实维护消费者合法权益，推动汽⻋产业⾼质量发
展。
⽀持企业围绕核⼼环节，建设⾼质量⼯业数据集，开发先进适⽤的垂域⼤

模型和各类⼯业专⽤模型
据央视新闻，会上介绍，党中央、国务院⾼度重视新⼀代智能制造发展。今年 9 ⽉召开的全国先
进制造业⼤会明确提出，要以新⼀代智能制造为主攻⽅向，加快推动制造业智能化、绿⾊化、
融合化发展。⽬前，⼯业和信息化部正在研究起草新⼀代智能制造的有关政策措施，加快推动
⼈⼯智能与制造业深度融合。
⼯业和信息化部将充分发挥我国⼈⼯智能创新活跃和制造业场景丰富的独特优势，重点做好四
⽅⾯⼯作。
⼀是梯度培育智能⼯⼚。⿎励基础级、先进级智能⼯⼚夯实数字化、⽹络化基础，在研发设
计、⽣产制造、运营管理、售后服务等环节深化⼈⼯智能应⽤。⽀持卓越级、领航级智能⼯⼚
探索新⼀代智能制造系统架构和未来制造模式，引领⾏业发展。
⼆是创新发展智能装备。⽀持企业研发智能⼯业⺟机、智能机器⼈、智能检测装备和智能控制
装备，提升⾃适应加⼯、柔性作业和在线检测能⼒，更好满⾜⾼精度、⾼可靠性⽣产要求。推
动智能⼯程机械、智能农机、智能医疗装备等研发应⽤，提升⾃主作业和智能服务能⼒，让装
备更加智能、产品更加好⽤，⽀撑相关⾏业智能化发展。

三是加快智能技术攻关和推⼴应⽤。⽀持企业围绕研发设计、⽣产制造等核⼼环节，建设⾼质
量⼯业数据集，开发先进适⽤的垂域⼤模型和各类⼯业专⽤模型，探索⼯业智能操作系统研
发，推动数据、模型、软件和装备相互适配、⾼效协同。围绕智能排产、参数调优、缺陷检
测、设备运维等场景，实施模数共振⾏动，稳妥开展⼯业智能体研发与应⽤验证，成熟⼀个、
推⼴⼀个。
四是深化智能制造国际合作。推动落实智能制造合作倡议，依托⾦砖等合作机制，加强智能⼯
⼚建设、标准规范体系等领域合作，⿎励企业、科研机构分享先进技术、解决⽅案和建设经
验，开展供需对接和⼈才培养，协助合作伙伴建设智能⼯⼚，共同推动制造业转型升级。
持续完善光伏新型储能等产能监测预警机制
据证券时报，⼯业和信息化部新闻发⾔⼈、运⾏监测协调局局⻓陶⻘表⽰，⼯信部将全⾯落实
全国先进制造业⼤会部署安排，着⼒做好四⽅⾯⼯作，全⼒实现年度预期⽬标，不断提升⼯业
经济运⾏质效。
⼀是着⼒扩⼤有效需求，完成新⼀轮⼗⼤重点⾏业稳增⻓⼯作⽅案年度⽬标，扩⼤优质商品和
服务供给，加快推进新⼀代通信⽹、算⼒⽹建设，部署⼀批标志性⼯程项⽬，⽀持出⼝企业加

### Page 3

强品牌建设，提⾼产品附加值。
⼆是着⼒打造创新引擎，统筹推进传统产业升级，新兴产业壮⼤，未来产业培育，促进先进制
造业扩容提质，实施新⼀轮制造业重点产业链⾼质量发展⾏动计划，深⼊开展国家⾼新区新赛
道培育⾏动，新认定⼀批专精特新 “ ⼩巨⼈ ” 企业和省级创新型产业集群；
三是着⼒培育增⻓动能，纵深推进⼈⼯智能 + 制造⾏动，新建设⼀批领航级卓越级智能⼯⼚，新
培育⼀批国家绿⾊⼯⼚、绿⾊⼯业园区、绿⾊供应链，实施第⼆批万兆光⽹试点，加快算⼒超
市建设，夯实数字技术底座和⽹络基础设施体系。
四是着⼒优化发展环境，深化重点领域⽆序⾮理性竞争综合整治，持续完善光伏新型储能等产
能监测预警机制，印发实施重点⾏业标准体系建设指南，以标准提升促进产业升级，加快加⼒
清理拖⽋企业账款。
适时研究进⼀步扩⼤增值电信业务开放试点
⼯业和信息化部信息通信管理局局⻓谢存表⽰，今年前三季度，信息通信⾏业发展总体平稳。
前三季度电信业务总量同⽐增⻓ 7.3% ，互联⽹业务收⼊ 1—8 ⽉同⽐增⻓ 8.9% 。算⼒设施⽅⾯，

截⾄ 2026 年 9 ⽉底，我国智能算⼒规模达 2592 EFLOPS ，同⽐增⻓ 146% ，有⼒⽀撑我国⼈⼯智
能产业快速发展，⾏业发展活⼒持续释放。
下⼀步，⼯业和信息化部将努⼒推动信息通信业提质升级。⼀是稳投资，把握新⼀代通信⽹建
设机遇，谋划实施⼀批具有战略性、引导性、突破性的重⼤⼯程项⽬，加快算⼒设施扩容提
质，为先进制造业发展夯实⽹络和算⼒底座；⼆是抓创新，前瞻布局 6G 技术创新，加强新⼀代
通信⽹标准体系建设，深化 5G ⼯业互联⽹应⽤创新，推进信息通信与⼈⼯智能融合创新，⼯业
互联⽹与⼈⼯智能融合赋能，⾼⽔平建设 5G+ ⼯业互联⽹融合应⽤城市试点，深度赋能先进制造
业；三是促开放，⽀持更多符合条件的企业申请卫星物联⽹业务商⽤试验，适时研究进⼀步扩
⼤增值电信业务开放试点。
加快设⽴国家中⼩企业发展基⾦⼆期
⼯业和信息化部总⼯程师王卫明表⽰，当前，中⼩企业尤其是⼩微企业，还⾯临着订单缺、成
本⾼、招⼈难等问题，还需要进⼀步加⼤⼒度⽀持中⼩企业持续健康发展。
⼯信部将建好、⽤好全国中⼩企业服务⼀张⽹，构建优质⾼效、直达快响的服务体系，深化⼤

中⼩企业协同融通发展，分⾏业探索建设专精特新赋能中⼼，加快设⽴国家中⼩企业发展基⾦
⼆期，带动更多耐⼼资本投向种⼦期初创期的企业，让更多潜⼒企业得到⽀持，安⼼成⻓。
将修订铜、铝等⾏业规范条例，坚决遏制低⽔平重复建设
下⼀步，⼯信部将持续在稳增⻓、促转型上下功夫，推动有⾊⾦属⾏业⾼质量发展，重点抓好
三⽅⾯⼯作。⼀是强化政策引领，落实原材料领域 “ ⼗五五 ” 规划，加强部⻔政策协同，推动优质
产品在新能源、⾼端装备、低空经济等领域的拓展应⽤；
⼆是加快产业升级，深⼊实施新材料重⼤专项，加快⾼端材料关键核⼼技术攻关，发挥有⾊⾦
属⾏业绿⾊低碳公共服务平台作⽤，制定《⼈⼯智能 + 有⾊⾦属⾏业实施指南》和有⾊⾦属⾏业
标准体系建设指南，引领⾏业 “ 向新、向绿、向智 ” 发展；

### Page 4

三是深化产能治理，实施重点产品产能⻛险监测预警，严格执⾏电解铝产能置换政策，严控氧
化铝、铜等冶炼新增产能，修订铜、铝等⾏业规范条例，坚决遏制低⽔平重复建设，营造公平
健康有序的市场环境。
以下是发布会内容：
国务院新闻办新闻局副局⻓、新闻发⾔⼈  贾惠丽 :
⼥⼠们、先⽣们，⼤家下午好。欢迎出席国务院新闻办新闻发布会。今天我们进⾏经济数据
例⾏发布，我们邀请到⼯业和信息化部总⼯程师王卫明先⽣，请他为⼤家介绍 2026 年前三季度
⼯业和信息化发展情况，并回答⼤家关⼼的问题。出席今天发布会的还有，⼯业和信息化部新闻
发⾔⼈、运⾏监测协调局局⻓陶⻘⼥⼠，⼯业和信息化部新闻发⾔⼈、信息通信管理局局⻓谢存
先⽣。
下⾯，⾸先请王卫明先⽣作介绍。
⼯业和信息化部总⼯程师  王卫明 :
⼥⼠们、先⽣们，新闻界的朋友们，⼤家下午好！⾸先，感谢⼤家⻓期以来对⼯业和信息化
事业的关⼼和⽀持。
今年以来，在党中央坚强领导下，⼯业和信息化系统深⼊贯彻党的⼆⼗⼤和⼆⼗届历次全会
精神，认真落实中央经济⼯作会议、中央政治局会议部署，深⼊贯彻总书记关于发展先进制造业

的重要指⽰精神和全国先进制造业⼤会部署，坚持稳中求进⼯作总基调，统筹稳增⻓、强创新、
促转型、优环境，积极应对⻛险挑战，⼯业和信息化⾼质量发展取得积极成效。
⼀是经济运⾏稳中提质。坚持供需两端协同发⼒，推动重点⾏业稳增⻓政策落地⻅效，扩⼤
优质⼯业品供给，⼯业经济平稳运⾏，⽣产、效益同步增⻓。前三季度，规模以上⼯业增加值同
⽐增⻓ 5.3% ，⼯业对经济增⻓的贡献率保持在 35% 左右。前 8 个⽉，规模以上⼯业企业利润总额
同⽐增⻓ 15.7% 。数字产业加快发展，收⼊同⽐增⻓ 14.6% 。
⼆是产业结构向新向优。加⼒实施制造业重⼤技术改造升级和⼤规模设备更新，传统产业焕
发新活⼒，新兴产业跑出加速度，未来产业布局抢先机。前三季度，规模以上⾼技术制造业增加
值同⽐增⻓ 14.3% ，装备制造业增加值占规模以上⼯业⽐重较上年年底提⾼了 0.8 个百分点，以
⼈⺠币计价机电产品和⾼新技术产品出⼝额占出⼝总值的⽐重分别较去年同期提升了 3.8 个和 4.9
个百分点。⼈形机器⼈等新产品加速⾛向市场，创新药领域成果不断涌现，今年前三季度我国获

批的创新药当中，国产占⽐达 80% ，肿瘤、精神、抗感染、痛⻛、罕⻅病等多领域药物研究取得
了重⼤突破。 6G 、脑机接⼝、⽣物制造等新赛道加速培育。
三是先进制造⾛深⾛实。以新⼀代智能制造为主攻⽅向，我们深⼊实施 “ ⼈⼯智能 + 制造 ” ⾏
动，先进制造业发展迈出新步伐。智能⼯⼚加快建设，累计建成了 1.3 万余家先进级智能⼯⼚、
500 余家卓越级智能⼯⼚。国产算⼒与⼤模型适配能⼒不断增强，在钢铁、汽⻋、医药等 20 个⾏
业形成了 1000 多个应⽤场景，遴选推⼴了 100 余项凝聚 “ ⽼师傅经验 ” 的⼯业智能体。绿⾊制造加
速发展，累计培育国家绿⾊⼯⼚ 8336 家，规模以上⼯业单位增加值能耗持续下降。个性化定
制、全⽣命周期服务等新模式不断拓展，先进制造业与现代服务业融合更深，推动制造业从 “ 卖
产品 ” 向 “ 卖⽅案 ” 升级，向价值链⾼端环节延伸。
四是发展环境持续优化。坚持精准服务企业，常态化开展制造业企业座谈交流，持续完善优
质中⼩企业梯度培育体系，着⼒解决企业急难愁盼问题。围绕整治重点⾏业⾮理性竞争，综合运

⽤市场化、法治化办法，维护公平健康市场竞争秩序。经过各⽅共同努⼒，汽⻋⾏业应付账款电
⼦凭证⽀付⽐例明显下降，国内主流光伏组件价格企稳。近期，国务院办公厅印发《关于加强中
⼩企业回款难问题治理有关⼯作的通知》，对依法治理中⼩企业回款难问题作出部署。 “ 回款难 ”
是不少中⼩企业⾯临的痛点，我们对此感同⾝受。今年以来，我们加快加⼒清理拖⽋企业账款，

### Page 5

推动规范账期、畅通资⾦循环，切实减轻企业负担。刚才讲的这些问题都是涉及企业、⾏业的重
⼤问题，我们⼀定会常抓不懈，下更⼤⼒⽓持续推动解决。
下⼀步，我们将深⼊贯彻落实全国先进制造业⼤会部署，推动存量政策提质增效、增量政策
加快落地，加快科技创新和产业创新深度融合，着⼒培育新动能，推动⼯业和信息化经济持续向
新向优向好发展，奋⼒完成全年任务⽬标，确保 “ ⼗五五 ” 开好局起好步。
我先介绍这些。下⾯，我和我的同事⾮常愿意回答⼤家关⼼的问题。

贾惠丽 :

中央⼴播电视总台央视记者 :
想问⼀下发⾔⼈，能否介绍⼀下前三季度我国中⼩企业发展情况？未来如何健全主动发现机
制、提升精准服务⽔平，⽀持更多有潜⼒的中⼩企业成⻓壮⼤？谢谢。
2026-10-20 15:29:10
王卫明 :
谢谢您的提问，这个问题由我来回答。中⼩企业量⼤⾯⼴，是经济韧性的重要⽀撑，也是培
育新质⽣产⼒的源头活⽔。今年以来，中⼩企业发展态势总体平稳、稳中有进。 1—8 ⽉，规上

⼯业中⼩企业利润总额同⽐增⻓ 13.8% ，为 2022 年以来同期最⾼⽔平；规上⼯业专精特新 “ ⼩巨
⼈ ” 企业利润总额同⽐增⻓ 22.2% ，好于全部规上⼯业企业，有⼒⽀撑了⼯业经济持续回升向
好。同时，我们也看到，中⼩企业尤其是⼩微企业还⾯临着 “ 订单缺、成本⾼、招⼈难 ” 等⼀些问
题，还需要进⼀步加⼤⼒度来⽀持中⼩企业持续健康发展。
刚才您提到了健全主动发现机制，这是完善优质中⼩企业梯度培育体系的重要⼀环。近年
来，⼀批初创企业凭借技术、市场等⽅⾯的特⾊优势快速成⻓，我们建⽴主动发现机制，就是要
在企业成⻓初期的 “ 萌芽阶段 ” 给予扶持，给予成⻓所需要的 “ 阳光⾬露 ” ，让它们⼼⽆旁骛地 “ 茁壮
成⻓ ” ，少⾛弯路。
⾸先要把好苗⼦识别出来。充分挖掘产业链供应链、技术创新、股权投资、赛事奖项、⼈才
团队等⼤数据信息，为潜⼒企业精准画像，把埋头搞研发、专⼼做产品的企业发掘出来。我们也
将加强政务数据共享，丰富数据来源渠道，迭代优化发现条件，⽤好⼈⼯智能技术，构建企业智

能画像，让更多潜⼒企业进⼊视野。
其次要把好资源贯通起来。各级中⼩企业主管部⻔将发现的潜⼒企业及时共享，实现部、
省、市、县四级数据贯通，通过数据的贯通带来政策和服务资源的贯通，共同为中⼩企业的发展
厚植成⻓⼟壤。建好⽤好全国中⼩企业服务 “ ⼀张⽹ ” ，构建优质⾼效、直达快享的服务体系。深
化⼤中⼩企业协同融通发展，分⾏业探索建设专精特新赋能中⼼，加快设⽴国家中⼩企业发展基
⾦⼆期，带动更多耐⼼资本投向种⼦期、初创期的企业，让更多潜⼒企业得到⽀持、安⼼成⻓。
最后要把好政策推⼴开来。落实《促进中⼩企业发展 “ ⼗五五 ” 规划》《优质中⼩企业梯度培
育管理办法》，实施⼈⼯智能中⼩企业创业⽀持计划，指导各地把潜⼒企业纳⼊培育范围， “ ⼀企
⼀档 ” 动态跟踪， “ ⼀企⼀策 ” 精准帮扶。主动靠前响应诉求，对潜⼒企业 “ 精准滴灌 ” ，缺资⾦的协
调融资，缺⼈才的对接引才，缺技术的匹配资源，把服务资源送到企业最需要的地⽅，助⼒它们
梯度成⻓壮⼤，⾛好专精特新发展道路。谢谢。
海报新闻记者 :

有⾊⾦属是重要的基础原材料，⼴泛应⽤于⼯业⽣产和⽇常⽣活。请问，今年前三季度我国
有⾊⾦属⾏业总体的发展情况如何？下⼀步将如何促进有⾊⾦属⾏业⾼质量发展？谢谢。

2026-10-20 15:37:50
